My grandmother spent the decade of the 1950s taking care of my grandfather who had become disabled after a series of strokes. She was in her 50s. I would describe her as a down to earth but high energy person. She worked full-time during that decade. And raised her child single-handedly. And cared  for her evermore disabled husband. Anyway, I didn’t know her until after my grandfather had died. After he died, there  were a couple of times that she could’ve had a partner. There were a couple of men who were interested in being in a relationship with her over the years. One  was the widow of a friend of hers, and the other was a man she met on a tour.  I wondered why she didn’t pursue either of these relationships. She preferred being on her own.  And it was probably as you describe - she probably just didn’t want to take care of someone else as they began to decline. She didn’t begin to decline until she was over 80.

@ Well, it's, maybe not like we'd hope. We wish for being able to share things or interests, maybe peacefully resolve old baggage. Maybe that happens for some and I really hope that it does. 
   Both the physical and mental load is substantial if you're the sole caregiver. If one or more you are taking care of have cognitive, mobility, or medical issues, this adds a significant level of care you'll need to provide. If you are also doing the cooking and cleaning, it takes an even higher toll on you.
   Mine just would not allow anyone (they'd run them off by the 3rd appointment) but me. If you can get reasonable support lined up or have siblings that pitch in, it could help a great deal and free you up to enjoy some of your time with them.

  --- reply (2025-04-22T22:52:46Z) ---
We had my Mum living with us for the last 7 years of her life (81-88). Thankfully it was a blessing. Our 2 sons (10 and 14 when she came) as well as my husband just loved having her with us. So did i. 
Only a few times right towards the end she sometimes became a bit confused. Each time when I explained to her what she'd become muddled about "Yes Mum I did bring you supper 😄", "Yes it is night time and no longer day. Maybe you closed your curtains earlier than usual?" - she would realise her mind wasn't functioning clearly, burst out laughing and say "Oh Darling, am I going bats?!" to which I always replied "Yes! I'm afraid so!".

The funniest was once she rang her bell imperiously at about 3am. I got the fright of my life. I ran down the passage, out of the back door, down the 3 steps (VERY nearly tripping and falling on my face)..to her flat.

I flung open her door. I had imagined that she must've had a fall and broken her hip, and somehow reached the buzzer to the main house.

Yet there she was, sitting straight up in bed, with her hands neatly in her lap.

"I want biscuits!" she declared. I went through to her little kitchen, made her a pot of tea and brought her 3 biscuits. She took a bite of the first one, a sip of her tea, then looked over the rim of her teacup with her big dark brown eyes. 

I was sitting on her bed.

Suddenly she said "Oh my darling, am I being ridiculous? Did I wake you? I am SO sorry!". 

We both laughed and laughed. She had become increasingly sweet-natured as she grew older. Not that she'd ever been mean. I had just not been close to her at ALL while growing up. 

So I was actually grateful for those last years.

We did have to get a carer in to help me to care for her 3x a week  in the morning.

I would not have managed to care for her if she was incontinent.

When my one brother had a dreadful aggressive cancer, after 4 mths we could not help him to the bathroom any more: he was 6'4", and he became extremely weak. He had to go into hospital. The last two months he spent in a wonderful old age home. 

I fully agree and understand what you are all saying here. My husband and I both turn 66 this year. We have spent the last year gradually re-painting the same house to sell it, sorting through our possessions, giving things away6, throwing others out, and I've sold a few things and been surprised at how much I earned for them.

I gave my precious guitar away 2 weeks ago to our painter - and he lit up like a beacon! He plays beautifully, and it was definitely the right thing to do.

We also both have many many books. We're only shedding a few of those at this point!
My husband has photographed and "digitised" ALL our photos, and our children's art, and his diaries (mine are private! 😄). 
He has shredded over 50 bags of papers, drawings etc. 
We have kept the VERY precious ones.

SO one day when we die, our sons will not have to spend months and months wading through everything, as my husband has been doing over years.

He doesn't resent it, but is motivated to avert our sons being landed in a similar stew. 

After we've moved to a smaller flat, if one of requires care, we have found a carehome that is not exorbitant and where the staff are apparently skilled and patient. 
Sounds perfect to me.
I just pray that until the day we die both of recognise our children and are able to express our love and say a proper goodbye ♥️❤️

Three of us adults cd n not

=== COMMENT 32 (2025-04-04T03:23:06Z, likes 91) ===
My mother turns 99 in two short months. I’m 71. She doesn’t live with me (thank Heavens!) but went into care after battling a couple of illnesses almost 3 1/2 years ago. I have looked after her for 17 years, getting her to all of her medical appointments, picking up groceries, helping her do her banking, etc and have been responsible for her healthcare and finances for 13 years since she first went into assisted living. She began showing signs of dementia a couple of years after that and also was experiencing more hearing loss. Now she is deaf, fully into dementia and in a wheelchair. Visiting her is no longer enjoyable and hasn’t been for a few years. She knows me but is oblivious to everything going on around her. I can’t imagine what it’s like for her to be living such a life. For me, it’s exhausting. Make no mistake, even though a family member isn’t looking after an elderly parent or family member personally, it is still emotionally and sometimes physically exhausting. In the ensuing years since my mother went into assisted living, I’ve had to pack up her belongings and move some items to my home and move a few items to her assisted living suite, empty her house of any other items in her store room, supervise the sale of her house, deal with the assisted living management and staff on my mother’s behalf (she didn’t want to do anything much, especially make big decisions, for herself) regarding her in-house supports etc. I’ve seen her through four surgeries and countless other appointments. I’ve sorted out and supervised financial and investment concerns for her, stored all manner of tax, finance, investment and medical information for her in multiple bankers boxes at my small home (which take up a lot of room!) and have run around getting her toiletries etc as well as visiting her regularly and meeting her in the emergency room whenever she’s had a fall and hurt herself. Now I have to meet the dental hygienist each time she comes to clean my mom’s teeth because my mom gets upset and agitated. If I’m there, my mom is calmer during the process. I have looked after my mom diligently for years, and I’ve lost count of how often my life has been put on the back burner in order to deal with her needs. Many people will know that when an elderly person has need of medical attention and such, that the carer has to make decisions and get the needs met because those needs can’t wait. Doctors, nurses, dental hygienists, Care-aids, or whoever, need prompt answers to their questions and decisions made by the person with Power of Attorney. So the family member in charge sacrifices their life regularly for the well being of the elderly person for whom they are responsible. I have my own home to look after as well as my own finances, taxes, grocery shopping etc to take care of but all of that isn’t taken into account by others. What I also know is that often, the designated carer gets little help if any from other family members. I’ve seen and heard about this time and again and it happened to me. My late brother did very little to help, rarely even offered. He was running a business with his wife but I often wondered it he kept working to avoid having to help with his mother and preferred to leave me carrying the full load alone. Am I tired out, emotionally and physically worn down? You bet I am! I’ve had my own health issues to deal with for the last 4 years or so and this makes looking after my mom even more difficult. Don’t ever let anyone tell you that it’s not so tough to care for a parent if your parent is in full care. That’s not only a lie, it’s an insult! 😒🇨🇦

  --- CREATOR (2025-04-04T14:36:09Z) ---
You’re not alone in feeling this way. So many people are expected to become caregivers to the very people who hurt them, neglected them, or showed them little care — and then everyone acts like it’s just your job now. That kind of emotional whiplash is real, and it cuts deep.

It makes sense that you’re feeling resentment. It doesn’t make you a bad person — it makes you a human being with a history. Your past matters. Your boundaries matter.

And please — don’t feel guilty for protecting your peace, your health, or your finances. You have the right to care for yourself. You are allowed to say ‘no,’ or ‘not like this,’ or ‘I need help.’ You can still be a compassionate person and draw a line to keep from drowning.

This isn’t about abandoning anyone — it’s about not abandoning yourself. And that matters too.

I have lived through everything you're talking about. I cared for my elderly mother while she went through hospice. My mom had moved in with us because she could no longer care for a house. I didn't realize until sometime later she was having cognitive issues as well as physical illness. When you have children, it's a given that they'll be in diapers for the first couple of years. When you have to put your parent in diapers, it's heartbreaking. You know they aren't going to grow out of it, and you watch the person that you looked up to and loved your whole life turn into this other person you don't know anymore. While my mother began to develop dementia, she remembered who I was up until the end. She may not have always been able to remember my name, but she remembered I was her daughter. Thank you for posting this. Sometimes you suffer tremendous guilt when you get upset with them or lose your patience. You can often resent being put in the position of caregiver because you don't always feel up to it. But I'm grateful every day that I was there for my mother. I wouldn't have traded those last couple of months for anything no matter how difficult it was.

=== COMMENT 66 (2025-04-03T10:56:24Z, likes 36) ===
I'm an occupational therapist and this is the most realistic video I've seen on this topic. In 2019, I relocated from California back to my hometown of Philadelphia to help care for my Father, who had dementia. I knew my mother wouldn't be able to handle him by herself. With the help of a friend of mine, who's a hospice nurse, we were able to keep him home until two weeks before he died in February 2022. Even with my healthcare experience, it was a challenging situation. My experience has been that the caregiving usually falls on the most responsible sibling. If you're lucky, you'll have two siblings who will help out. Everyone else looks the other way, living their lives and pretending that life is great. If the family is dysfunctional, all the cracks will show. I think the hardest thing is you don't know how long it will go on for. Caring for the elderly shows just how broken American culture is. It's all good till you can't care for yourself. Then it's a different story. People don't want to face this reality and therefore it falls on one or two responsible people to do all the work.

  --- CREATOR (2025-04-03T12:21:34Z) ---

=== COMMENT 67 (2025-04-02T19:52:20Z, likes 93) ===
I took care of my mother with dementia for almost 10 years, along with her significant other for six years.  He was very large, in a wheel chair, no dementia. He helped and cooperated. === COMMENT 70 (2025-04-07T03:19:57Z, likes 304) ===
MUST READ!     So true.  There are so many problems.  Here is a typical day for me:
Keep in mind I am an only child and there is no other family.  I am in my 50's, the parent in their 90's.  We do get help from the State and SS so I am able to be here.
1.  They wake up at any odd hour yelling to go to the bathroom.

2.  I go in and have to lift the person up from behind in order to get up out of bed.  They need help getting up at other times also.
3.  They need help getting night clothing off before going to the bathroom, like leg warmers, thick socks, and so on.
4.  They sit up then and gather a few items from the nightstand.  There are 3 glass cases, each with a different item that they feel they must take to bed and back with them.... so an invisible enemy does not steal them.  Paranoia and delusions: yes.
5.  They use a walker to bulldoze their way around and then get to the toilet.  They have severe OCD so they take forever with each task.  They put toilet paper on the toilet seat as a seat cover, even though I, the daughter, am the only other person who uses it.  They sit and do their business.  They wear several layers.  An underwear, with a pad.  A set of folded paper towel.  A diaper.  Two pairs of pants.  One pair of socks.  If any one of these is soiled, it all has to come off which they can't do by themselves.  Usually at least one of them is so it all needs to go off and some in the wash.  They sit there for a time with a mirror, picking at little things.  Finally they get up, dropping most of the toilet paper on the seat to the floor.  They grab another small piece of toilet paper... to flush the toilet!  Oh, and that falls to the ground as well.  They get up and shuffle over to the sink while I pick up all the pieces of wasted TP with a grabber.  They wash their hands.... for half an hour.  Then, they dry their hands... for another half hour.  But... you can't rush them.  The faucet handles are slathered in soap so later I wipe them down.  They shuffle back to the bedroom where I put all the clean clothes back on and new sets of the items.  
6.  The bed... has about six blankets on that are slippery so the bed is a mess each morning.  They need so many layers of everything and we take sweaters, polar hats, shawls and stuff to the living room when all else is done.
7.  They sit and turn on the space heater.... which is on a LOT.  Even though it's not that cold.  It is also expensive.  I fix the meal and do the dishes several times a day.  They want tea... again.  It got cold... again.  It is in a thermus, but they forget to put the lid on and would rather have it off... because... it's too hot.  
8.  After a meal sometimes they get diarrhea.  And it is no small amount.  They make it to the toilet as fast as they can, but it still gets everywhere.  They can't clean themselves, or the mess left behind that is all over the toilet and the floor.  They can even slip in it.  So I get to deal with it all, after putting a fresh set of everything on already a little while ago.  So it's a new set of everything after the major wipe down and cleaning, sanitizing the area.
9.  They make a mess everywhere.  They leave little pieces of trash here and there.  I do all the housework as it is, laundry, etc. etc.  I do bring blankets and warm drinks.  I am fine with that and even enjoy it and don't complain about the typical needs, but it's the attitude that is the killer.
10.  They insist on putting their contact lenses in.  They lose one.  But it wasn't them, someone is sneaking in and stealing them.  They want to buy more.  They spent a lot on lenses as it is.  They have old ones but with damage, they insist on using them anyway, they scream in pain, can't get them out, finally do, one gets lost, and it starts all over again.
11.  They used to leave things on the stove that would burn due to early dementia.  After I'd gone to bed.  Trying to get them in bed before me was impossible.  If I go on errands I worry about this also.  Putting things in the oven 'to dry,' leaving faucets running while doing hand laundry and the sink would run over.  One day the house was filled with smoke.  The only thing that sinks in is if you get really mad and yell.  If they were bed ridden, my life would have been easier!
12.  They don't want a traditional bath.  
13.  Forget about any sort of vacation or travel, decent day off, etc.
14.  They have their own version of reality.  If they can't push it on you, they get hostile and attack you.
15.  They do foolish things and fall down.  They have conditions that could lead to broken bones with a fall.  I tell them but they don't listen.  They can't get up by themselves.  I am strong and they are thin so I am able to get them up.  But it's not always easy.  Sometimes they don't want to use the walker.  The cane is not enough.  But they have a 'can do' attitude.  Great.
16.  You can't tell them anything, they won't listen to you... because "you are their child and they are the parent" is in their mind forever.
17.  We had to change something at the bank to keep the homecare hours.  The parent kept arguing with me about it even though the amount was my money.  I finally lost it and yelled and then they cooperated and it turned out okay.
18.  I am always worried they will say something wrong at the reassessments due to dementia and we will lose the hours, like "oh no, I don't need any help with that, or this..." etc.   They are very 'independent,' and stubborn and delusional about their abilities.  And that situation has happened, causing a loss of some hours.
19.  Now hallucinations have begun and there are mysterious people in their bedroom at night.
20.  Getting them in bed is now easier due to physical decline and lack of enthusiasm for other things, and the person had always been a night owl.  So this was a big win.
21.  With dementia people argue, get aggressive, don't want to cooperate, and in this case there is also mental illness.
22.  There was an illness earlier that caused terrible behavior that was toxic and verbally abusive.  I think it would even go physical but they know I am stronger so they don't dare try anything stupid.  The attitude would get better and worse.  At one point last winter, and we get snow, it was so bad I did not want to run into them in the hallway and I had to go to the bathroom so I went into the backyard with freezing temperatures just to empty my bladder.  The snow cleaned up after me.  I would rather shiver than deal with the bad attitude.
23.  On top of all this, people still look down on caregivers.  There is the attitude of 'just' a caregiver, like you are just a servant, maid, slave, second class citizen, etc.  It's just some lowly job in their eyes like a janitor, cleaning lady, etc.
24.  In public they can turn on the charm, so they always tell me how 'cute' my parent is and so on, because they know so much more about it of course.  All my saintly sweetness goes unnoticed by them most of the time.  Patience... the patience that is needed, the nerves that are shredded, the stress... my BP went up, I have other health conditions due to the stress that I haven't had in over a decade, and so on.  
25.  It just goes on and on and on.  The worst combination is when they are still physically able but not mentally.  That's when they can start wandering outside at night and stuff.  That... did not occur.   There are symptoms of nearing death but in this case it is drawn out longer than the time estimates given.  For some it goes on for decades depending on when dementia sets in.  The worst aggravation is the bad behavior.  The accusations.  The irrationality and hostility and even harassment.  That stuff is what takes such an emotional toll.  I could live with the rest, but it is due to that that I am at my wits end!  Now I even have to worry about the social safety nets breaking due to doge and then we would be stranded.  Because this wasn't already stressful enough.  Kids grow up pretty quick, but this can last decades and only gets worse.  The physical demands, time demands, need for supervision, they can hardly be left alone, managing them such as redirecting their attention away from something setting them off, etc.  It's like having a special needs kid mentally and physically who thinks they are in charge.  No one understands that I am praying for this to end.  Once life gets to that point it is no longer a quality life.  They are human and I don't believe in euthanasia or stuff, but I would not want to get to this point.  There is no money for a nursing home either.   When conversations used to be a thing, all they would talk about was the bad stuff, the bad people in their life and what they did in the past, which I heard hundreds of times, and it wasn't even all true.  Yes.. the little joys!
There is probably more that I am forgetting but you get the picture by now I would imagine and you probably want to forget it and run.  Lol

  --- reply (2025-04-09T15:02:00Z) ---
My sister and I had to split care between our parents because my father and I were estranged from years back. She took on his care and I'm pretty sure she thought I was going to have it worse with our mom. Mom died suddenly at home, probably from a slow suicide because she refused to eat and was again engaging in the substance abuse that had been off and on since she was young. Our dad developed Alzheimer's. I truly believe my sister was on her way to having a mental breakdown when she was finally able to get him into a dementia unit at a local nursing home. Not only was she caring for him but also having to take care of the third wife because this person had decided years earlier she no longer wanted to get out of bed each day. My father had been caring for her since that time until he was no longer able to do so. They both went to the same facility. My father, who had been a social introvert for most of his life suddenly turned into the life of the party the two years he was there. He thrived in the environment, probably because he was eating regular meals and taking his medications like he should. He passed away in 2023 after developing a septic UTI. The staff told my sister they knew something was wrong when he left a party early to go lie down in his room. To them, that was uncharacteristic behavior. To us, we would have just thought his dementia was easing off 😂. Never, ever, ever feel guilty for putting your parent somewhere they will be safer. Do not allow anyone to tell you they would never do this to their parents unless you know they didn't. I did my best to support my sister through this and today we have a better relationship then we had as kids simply because we were able to overcome past grievances and move forward when we were faced with these situations. But it isn't like that for everyone. We were just lucky and blessed. Not everyone has that support

=== COMMENT 80 (2025-04-21T09:08:25Z, likes 16) ===
I'm Korean and I am suprised to learn that the western people are dealing with the same problem. In Korea, many elderly in need of constant care are hospitalized for long term in a 'care for the elderly' hostpital. The cost is from 1400 to 2200 dollars a month, depedning on the situation. (prvite room or shared room). My mother-in-law passed away after staying five years in that hopstital. She was tube-fed and was changed in the last two years. My 83-year-old mom dreads the most of the possiblity of her ending up in the care hospital. But most Koreans are accepting the fact that the care hospital is where they have to go eventually. Those who cannot afford the expense take care of their parents by themselves. You got a new subscriber!

=== COMMENT 81 (2025-04-02T22:55:15Z, likes 43) ===

I am a caregiver to my parents. I am a 59 year old divorced mother of 2 grown boys. I had been an empty nester for about 2 years when I was asked to retire early from my teaching career, sell my house, and move to another state to care for my mom with dementia and my dad with cancer. I have been here 19 months now. I left a vibrant area to move to a rural area. I miss my old life. I miss my own space. I miss my friends. I had a nestegg from the sale of my home, but it is quickly dwindling away with one still in college and no paycheck coming in. Because I left my job early, I can not draw retirement until age 60. It has been hard! Dementia is such a terrible disease. My mother was so energetic and so full of life. She did everything right - she took care of herself - never drank or smoked, walked 10 miles a day, and ate healthy. To see this happen to her is so cruel. Why take care of yourself?! It is hard for me to even remember who she was. My dad was the same as mom, always eating healthy and very athletic - never drank or smoked. I get to watch him fight cancer and the evils of chemo. My kids don't come visit anymore because this isn't their home, and they say it is "depressing." I have decided that I want to be in a nursing home if I get to this point. I also want to leave my entire body to science - no burial and no funeral! This Christmas was the first one that I had without my kids. I wonder how long my life will be on hold and if I will even have a life after this.

=== COMMENT 91 (2025-04-10T20:25:00Z, likes 27) ===
I was 48 in 2018 (daughter) when my father's illness reached the point where he could not toilet by himself anymore.  He did not have dementia.  He had his full mental capacities and in a way, that made it worse.  Having to change his diaper was extremely traumatic for me.  I only had to do it on the weekends when I went to his home to give his wife (not my mother) a break.  My brother was a truck driver and was never around.  I told my brother about it, how it felt like a violation and he paid for home health care workers to come in for several days a week for several hours and that was a game changer for me and my father's wife. Having them meant that during the week, his wife could get more time to herself and to do other things and only had to change him/bathe etc. about two days a week instead of five (she was in her mid 60s) and I didn't have to change him anymore on the weekends because the home health workers were there to do it.  Since my dad was in his full right mind, it was the hardest thing to tell him that I just couldn't do it.  I would do everything else on the weekends: cook meals for the week, clean the whole house, wash dishes, go grocery shopping, run other errands, but I just couldn't change his diaper anymore.  He said he understood and I hope he really did.  He passed away in 2019.

Not sure how things work in the States but in the UK, if you have less than @£23k in savings, social services picks up the tab for care costs BUT they claim it back when your house is sold if you own one. The only workaround is to put your property in trust at least 7 years before you think you might need to be funded. People who have no savings and no property get free care. People who have worked hard to own their own home to leave something to their kids don't ... even if they've paid a ton of taxes over their lifetime. It's a very unequal system but who said life is fair?! At least there's a safety net in this country which is something but we have the same problems here with staff shortages.

=== COMMENT 137 (2025-04-06T06:32:04Z, likes 14) ===
As someone reaching this stage of being elderly and noticing the decline, I  don't want to be burdensome. My kids are already so busy and it just wouldn't be fair to put my care on them like that. 
In a more natural state of being, the decline and the end wouldn't take so long, but end of life has been extended beyond what it used to be, and that's not always a good thing for anyone!

It is beyond hard for a loving child to deal with all of that, as well as the pressures of their spouse and children, and often work too. This is an excellent video which hopefully will help release the guilt for so many. I've had my life, and my children don't deserve the added strain on their already over- busy, over- stressful lives. Once the caring reaches beyond loving care to medical care, then it isn't wrong to hand that care over to the professionals.  That way, you can come visit and enjoy your time with your loved one, dignity intact all round. ❤

  --- reply (2025-04-02T21:59:51Z) ---
All I can say is they CAN be overruled, and should be. Forget the security cameras, just get her admitted whether she likes it or not. My sister and I spent months trying to persuade my mother to go into a rest home, which thankfully in NZ is paid for by the state. She absolutely refused. After one particularly big argument she caved in & the irony is that she was very happy there. Rest homes always have something going on, they bring a tea trolley around, there's occasional entertainment and caregivers are always popping in & out. The care wasn't perfect but as I lived close by I was able to take her out a couple of times a week, have her at my place in front of the fire with the dog on her lap etc. That went on for years. In the end she did get both cantankerous and incontinent and I realised I could simply never have managed her on my own. I don't think any single person or couple can. It takes a team & rest homes have the staff, even if it's not as many as they should. The last couple of years were not pretty but I feel it was the only real option. So you guys need to make the decision now & do it against her will. All of you will be happier for it, including her.

  --- reply (2025-04-04T14:03:41Z) ---
@[member] If it's any consolation, I was in the same position. Even to considering cameras as Mum was letting strangers into the house.  Mum lived 9hrs from me, refused to move closer, and wanted me to move up there to look after her. I said no, my job, life and my children's lives were back at home. She wasn't socialising with anyone and at 88 she wasn't doing anything, but she still refused to move, or accept help from anyone else but me and my children . Eventually, life takes its natural course. After 2 years of having to travel up every time she fell (about every 5 weeks), stay up there 2 weeks to get her and the house clean again, get her well fed, set up daily carers that she'd Cancel as soon as I'd gone...she eventually had a more serious fall. This time, during Covid, after an 8hr wait for an ambulance whilst I travelled up (a neighbour was with her) they wouldn't take her to hospital. After staying a week being woken up several times a night to get on her the commode with my son ( who had also taken time off work to help me look after her) and realising she couldn't be left alone this time - I called her doctor in who convinced her she needed respite care. After that, things fell into place. The dementia she'd been assessed with but denied actually helped her deal with the process in the end. It was difficult getting her down to my home town, an expensive ambulance was hired. And sorting her house to get it sold, then selling and emptying it was a nightmare from such a distance, but she's safe and well looked after now. However, that 2 years of constant stress, travelling, looking after her to the point of exhaustion and pain (I had arthritis, now severe) broke my health, and I had to give up my job. I'd advise you to be firm with her. A health professional told her she was being selfish but she refused to accept it. My children, who have always adored her, feel resentful sometimes (then guilty for feeling that) because of the impact it had on me. 
Mum is still alive, existing as a non comprehending, non communicating, doubly incontinent shell. They're in denial, thinking they'll stay active and then one night just die peacefully in their sleep. People live longer now, but the last years aren't in good health. As a loving daughter you think you should do it all, you want to accommodate all their wishes. Maybe you can, but the odds are you won't be able to. Showing her this video might be a step too far but you need to point out the reality, before it breaks you and possibly the relationships surrounding.

=== COMMENT 151 (2025-04-12T22:17:07Z, likes 5) ===
This is so on the mark.  The last twelve months were some of the hardest of my life.  I am 67, drawing my state pension but still having to work full time (although I work at home) and a year ago today (12th April 2024) my mum had a bad fall (this was the third, that I know of, she hadn't told me about the previous ones).  She fell in the bathroom and couldn't get up.  My partner and I couldn't move her and it took him and the two ambulance staff to get her into a wheelchair.  She spent 3 1/2 months in hospital, ending up doubly incontinent, bed bound and completely dependent on others.  There was no way she could return to her own home so she came to live with me.  It was ok at first, but there had been signs when she was in hospital that all was not well and some of her behaviour was extremely difficult to deal with (we now know she has Lewy body dementia).  There was just me to look after her (my partner and don't live together and he had to be away for three months), with two carers coming in four times daily for a maximum of two hours in total.  I couldn't move her or turn her, or change her on my own (because she couldn't help me and it needed two people) although I did my best.  I found some of the things she did so disgusting that I found myself trying to avoid going into the room - or hoping she would be asleep when I did have to go in.  There was no-one to sit with her while I took the washing to be dried (I don't have space for a dryer so was using my partner's), nor when I went shopping, so I had to leave her alone at those times and I couldn't always drop what I was doing to see what she wanted when she called me, which made me feel guilty and sometimes angry too because so much was expected of me.  When I did have to go out I had to be back in time to let the carers in because giving them keys or access would have caused problems with my contents insurance.  There were also people turning up at odd times to do reviews on her and never with an appointment (only one appointment was made and the doctors gave the District Nurse the wrong time so she was an hour early.  I had one DN turn up unannounced at 3pm on a Sunday afternoon (I was collecting the washing and had to rush back).  In the end it became too much for me.  She slept much of the day and then, of course, couldn't sleep at night.  I was so tired, and I lost count of the nights I cried myself to sleep.  My cousin rang me and said she could tell from my voice that I was cracking under the strain because I just didn't sound like me any more.  I was beginning to resent mum being there, which made me feel even more guilty and wasn't how I'd envisaged what would be her final months, and I was in a downward spiral.  On 7th January she moved into a care home, where she is receiving nursing care because of her needs.  They are surprised I managed for as long as I did because they are trained professionals and they find her challenging.  She wants to go home and can't understand why she can't but she isn't safe to be on her own.  It's the last thing either of us wanted but in the end there was no other way.  I go over twice a week to see her, and it can be difficult, but with the distance between us and my knowing that other people who understand her needs better than I could are responsible for her well-being, my love for her that I was in danger of losing has returned.  It's like she is two people though, my mum and a stranger that I don't know and who I wish would just get out of her body and leave us both alone.

All I can say to anyone faced with looking after a loved one like this is that it's not for everyone, and if it's not for you please don't do it, let the professionals do it and don't feel guilty about that.  I love my mum, and I looked after her for five months, but it nearly destroyed me.  You have to recognise when you can't go on and accept it.

=== COMMENT 152 (2025-04-06T08:11:07Z, likes 7) ===
It's also important to talk about the decline in elderly independence and physical health overall. It's not normal to not be able to do anything yourself and take loads of medicine. It's completely possible to live on your own  and be flexible and mentally well in your 80's and 90's. It's sad that our lifestyles and environment creates these problems.

  --- CREATOR (2025-04-06T11:49:11Z) ---
I completely agree. It is possible to live independently and stay mentally and physically strong well into our 80s and 90s. I’ve seen it too, and it’s incredibly inspiring.
At the same time, I also know that sometimes people face things way outside of their control—health conditions, injuries, or systemic issues that don’t come from lifestyle choices alone. So I never want to be judgmental about what aging ‘should’ look like.
But you’re absolutely right. There’s power in personal responsibility, and I do believe we have more influence over our long-term health than many of us realize. Thank you for bringing this perspective into the conversation. It’s such an important one.

=== COMMENT 168 (2025-04-03T10:38:28Z, likes 20) ===
The best thing people can do for their children is to take responsibility for their own health. Eat a healthy diet. Exercise to maintain strength and mobility. The consequences of your piss poor lifestyle choices shouldn’t be a burden put on your children.

  --- reply (2025-04-03T15:57:11Z) ---
I feel the same way when I listen to stories about wayward adult children who are always in financial need, want to move back home, and worse, are emotionally and financially draining drug addicts, alcoholics, or have a criminal nature that zeros in on their parent's finances. Why should parents pay for their kid's college? Their weddings? Be an on-call babysitter?  It works both ways.

 In general, all adults aged 18+ must adopt a lifestyle to make themselves as independent as possible. I speak as an adult child and one who works in healthcare and have witnessed some wicked disregard for the elderly from various people, i.e., the public at large, the government, their healthcare providers, and their family members. Yet after their death, everybody wants a piece of what is left, especially the government and the family. 

This is not a simple black-and-white issue. It is a swirling cloud of endless gray. EVERYONE is one accident or unexpected life-altering diagnosis away from losing their health and independence and financial ruin. 

 The advice in this video is sound and necessary and will work well for some families. Unfortunately, life is complex and messy, and most scenarios will require a lot more finesse to work well for both sides. One thing  NEVER to do is abandon your elderly loved one in a facility. You need to check in on their care regularly. You need to be heard from and seen by staff. Even people with dementia feel physical pain and should not be left soiled and unkempt. Adult children may not be doing DIRECT care, but they still need to be part of their parent's life and death with dignity.

The stent has given her almost 4 more years of even worse health issues, and the assisted living facility I had to place her in is almost $10K/month for her rent and care. If she had a quality of life post stent I would have been all for the surgery, but her cardiologist was right all along. And even with assisted living you still have to be present and involved, do supply runs, etc. My mother will be 90 in July, and my anxiety is always through the roof! She's on at least three pages of medications. Without them, I'm sure she would have passed much earlier.

GREED GREED sets in more than anything else when a parent is dying or has died..  in my case that is what took over with my siblings…I had no qualms about taking care of her but it is difficult.  If you are not up for it do not do it…but be kind and find a good facility…DO NOT let GREED get in your way of a good care home.  Remember THEY raised you, fed you, nurtured you, etc.  Remember you only get ONE set of parents in this life, hoping they are wonderful, so be kind and loving in their dying days….Hugs 🙏🙏👍👍💜💜💜

=== COMMENT 234 (2025-04-12T21:57:01Z, likes 2) ===
I'm a 76 year old woman in declining health and mobility.  I don't want to burden my family, but I'm scared of nursing homes and rehabilitation facilities.  I've seen too many that were poorly staffed, both in numbers and skills/training.   Many are staffed with immigrants who can't speak English, and therefore can't communicate with the patients.  I personally know of multiple occasions where  staff stole the patient's belongings.  My daughter is a nurse in a rehab facility and she comes home with horror stories about staff failing to give medications as directed, or neglecting to give them at all, often with serious consequences. And don't get me started on covid horror stories where loved ones were not allowed visitation.   One elderly man I know of  stood outside his wife's window in freezing weather so he could see her.  So what's the answer? Most of us can't afford live-in or home health care.  No one ever said we were entitled to an easy life.  In fact, for the vast majority,  life is hard. But that's what shapes us.

=== COMMENT 271 (2025-04-02T19:33:08Z, likes 52) ===
I am a 49 year old male who looks after my 90 year old mother who has vascular dementia. I quit work to be her full time carer. I am entirely alone - I have no siblings and my relatives say that they don't care about me as I am not a member of their immediate family. I often feel burnt out and stressed out but I wouldn't change it for anything. My mother looked after me for the first 42 years of my life so there is no way that I will ever let her go into a nursing home. After a stay in hospital, the hospital refused to let her come back home with me (I found out that they did this to every elderly patient on the ward, they had an 'arrangement' with a nursing home) . They dumped her in a nursing home - but I used my Power of Attorney to rescue her and get her back home with me. She was always there for me growing up so I am here for her. Everybody remember this - you may feel frustrated now but one day your loved ones will be gone and you would do anything and give away everything you have for just one more moment with them - so cherish them now. Don't grumble or be resentful - do it with a smile on your face and thank God that they are still here. Which would you rather be doing? - working for some bastard of a boss in an office or looking after the person who cared for you and gave you life? At the end of the day there is nothing more meaningful. To be honest, if I had children and they wanted to put me in a home I would feel completely betrayed. Any parents who are able to still make decisions - cut the buggers out of your will if they don't look after you!

  --- CREATOR (2025-04-04T12:40:00Z) ---
That happens so often and it’s incredibly frustrating. People with dementia can “perform” for a few minutes, and professionals sometimes miss what’s really going on. If it’s safe to do, consider video recording short clips of the aggressive or confused behavior. It can be really helpful to show the full picture to doctors, social workers, or senior services.

For help, you can contact your local Area Agency on Aging go to Eldercare.acl.gov and enter your ZIP code to find resources near you. You can also ask your parent’s doctor for a referral to a neurologist or geriatric specialist for a full cognitive evaluation. You deserve support and you’re not alone in this. You are right too that it will only get worse. Hang in there.

We have been taking care of my mother in law for 9.75 years.  She is now 98. === COMMENT 346 (2025-04-02T22:43:27Z, likes 5) ===
Thanks, for the most honest picture of care giving I've heard, in a long time. The government policy, where I live, is to download care, to the families, without any concern, for the physical, psychological and financial burden. Even if you have a POA, good luck putting them in a nursing home or even accessing crappy "at home" services, if the patient can still utter the word "no." I am caring for my mother, with Alzheimer's.  She came to the disease, with a bevy of serious, untreated mental illness.  It's impossible to describe how combative, abusive, stubborn and reckless she is.  I honestly wish I was dead. I will NEVER do this, to my children.  In my living will, it states clearly, that if I'm cognitively impaired, my family is empowered to place me in residential care, notwithstanding any objections, from my demented self. I have also stipulated that, if I can't manage my own food, I am not to be spoon or tube feed.  You have to write this stuff down, when you're competent.  Once you have dementia, you probably won't recognize your own deficits.

$720.00/day for home health aids based on the charged rate of $30.00/hr for the home health agency we are using for my mom's care. We are charged time and half for holidays and extra charge for night shift.  I don't know how much they get paid but that is how much we are charged per hour for her care. I guess this rate depends on the amount of care required ie. skilled care , where a person resides (cost of living varies ), and other factors. We are charged the same rate regardless if my mom is sleeping or not for whatever shift or hours that she needs.

We were looking into a really nice memory care facility in our area in which you actually have to be private pay there for  a certain period of time before they would accept a resident as medicaid. === COMMENT 351 (2025-05-02T21:33:33Z, likes 1) ===
I’m a young disabled person and the unfortunate truth is that even if you put your family in a facility, you need to be involved in their care. Don’t think for a second that these facilities are going to mind their P’s and Q’s when it comes to caring for your loved ones! Check for bed sores, bruises, see how they’re eating. Are they having enough bowel movements? What’s their bloodwork look like? Do they have a roommate? Get to know their family and look out for each other.

=== COMMENT 352 (2025-04-02T19:33:57Z, likes 4) ===
One sentence. BUY long term care insurance so the nursing home does not take your assets or make you sell your home to pay for the care.  Nursing homes are NOT covered under your health insurance.  The health care given will be, but the stay at the home is not.  Or you can put your home in both your and your kids name long before that happens, since Medicaid does a 4 year look back period.. to keep the nursing home from taking your home.  Same with your bank account.

  --- reply (2025-04-02T22:13:28Z) ---
Ah, interesting. In the UK it's 7 years look back (gulp!) but def worth putting property into trust retaining a lifetime interest kind of thing

  --- reply (2025-04-11T16:52:00Z) ---
If it is both your names, the nursing home can take your parents share. You have to absolutely trust your children to put it in their names. Parents may need their home for nursing home care. It's their home.Unless you are taking care of them, you should not expect an inheritance.

=== COMMENT 407 (2025-04-20T17:35:29Z, likes 1) ===
So the problem is if your parent doesn't have the financial ability to move into some kind of assisted living situation - then their only option is to stay in their home as long as possible. Many are right on the border line making maybe $100/month too much to qualify for Medicaid (about $2800/mo) and yet there's no way they can afford the $5000 to $8000/month or more required to live in various levels of assisted living. Selling a home can sometimes help, but often they still owe on it or they've borrowed against it to remain afloat. And often major investment needs to be made to update a house so it sells (lots of deferred maintenance) so you might have to try to come up with $50k or more just to get the house sellable. Meanwhile the elderly parent has to live somewhere while awaiting the money from the sale.

Get a caregiver everyone says. They have one but she doesn’t live there. My dad qualifies for 20 hours a week. This does relieve me of many things but they will intentionally schedule appointments when the caregiver isn’t there. 
Despite setting boundaries - they never stick.
I wish I was the person who could walk away. 
I finally retired and now I am living in this horrible situation.

=== COMMENT 418 (2025-12-27T15:39:23Z, likes 1) ===
We moved in with my parents believing it would be a supportive situation for everyone, especially as my mum has moderate dementia and my dad struggles with everyday tasks. I expected it to be challenging, but I didn’t anticipate how emotionally draining it would become.

Over time, boundaries and privacy have slowly eroded. Simple things like noise late at night, lack of respect for personal space, and dismissive reactions when concerns are raised have created constant tension. The hardest part has been seeing this spill over onto our 10-year old daughter, who is regularly made to feel guilty or shamed for not giving the level of attention they seem to expect from her.

She’s a child, yet there’s an unspoken pressure for her to perform emotionally to sit, engage, and meet adult needs even when she’s tired, unwell, or uncomfortable. Watching a child absorb that kind of expectation has been incredibly difficult, especially when attempts to gently set boundaries are met with tutting, resentment, or the reminder that “it’s our house.”

What this experience has taught me is that illness and struggle don’t automatically create empathy, and trying harder or being more accommodating doesn’t always improve things sometimes it makes the imbalance worse. This video really resonated because it put words to something I’ve been living with quietly: the slow exhaustion that comes from living in an environment where responsibility flows one way, but respect doesn’t.

In the very last stage of his dementia, my sister (POA) finally put him in a nursing home after discussing it with all the siblings. It was a long 20 or so years!!!!  My mom had a massive stroke on the day my dad died and then she passed away just 31 days after my dad from all the stress she endured while taking care of my dad.  We should have put him in a nursing home a lot sooner than later.  My mom had medical issues that we did not realize she had because we were all focused on dad for so long and NOT her.  She never told us she was having any significant health issues.  She kept her problems to herself.  So sad that we did not look out for our mom better than what we did!!!

=== COMMENT 448 (2025-04-13T19:25:23Z, likes 2) ===
I absolutely agree.  This hits very close to home.

My mother did finally agree to go into a care home, and lived there 7.5 years, gradually declining into dementia, and for the last 3 years needing nursing care, in bed. I felt so guilty I couldn’t do it, but I have chronic illness, so I’m glad I didn’t try, knowing I couldn’t but still feeling guilty. Then the chief nurse said not to be, that no one could do it alone.  She reminded me they need a whole team, even though they do 12  hr shifts.  No way. That helped me to be realistic.  
Also that the toileting and bathing is better done by a carer (but preferably not a young man for an elderly woman! - that generation cares and so will I!). because that’s going to destroy the mother /daughter relationship and even more the mother/son. 
You’re so right about fecal impaction - constipation is an absolute nightmare to manage. 
I went to help Mum most days, and latterly every day and that exhausted me, 3-4 hr shifts. I managed the gaps that seemed to get forgotten, made sure things got done, fed her, cut and styled her hair, nails, shaved her, bathed her, kept her interested and clothed, supplied and amused, and endless conversation. When I was there the staff left me to it. I hope I did a good job, but I’m still exhausted, just from that. After she died I am executor and have her affairs to deal with, but at least they’re familiar because I managed them for her for over 15yrs!  I’m in my 70s and she was 92. 
Now I have nightmares about how my own end will be the same, fearing that my memory is going, and what it will be like to be left alone for hrs on end lying on a bunched up wet nighty with a tv channel I don’t want to watch.  Or there’s no signal. But never mind because I can’t change the batteries in my hearing aids by myself and where are my glasses? I could really murd3r a cuppa and the food is so boring. And worst of all, if your spouse is still alive, and also in care, why aren’t we together anymore?
Mum used to ask why she couldn’t come home with me, hating her fate. She imagined a rocking chair by the fire, helping with the veggies and talking my hind leg off. Such images would have her still my own age or younger, and me half her age, without grandchildren to fetch from school. In the end my sons moved away so the other grandmas could do the childminding, which is so sad. 
The world has gone mad. We live too long, often dissociated from one another, and start our families too late. And perhaps needing two wages to pay our way doesn’t help us cope either.
A place in a care home costs an absolute fortune, yet the carers are on a basic wage. 
Perhaps I won’t get the pneumonia protection when it’s offered. They used to call that the old dear’s friend - it takes you quietly Home.

=== COMMENT 452 (2025-05-09T21:15:07Z, likes 1) ===
Thanks for this video ! Lately I have seen a number of videos of people taking care of elderly, usually demented, parents at home. The vibe is how interesting and almost "fun" it is. It is not ! It is backbreaking, depressing and exhausting. My fear is with the current focus on cutting Medicaid ( over 60% of nursing home patients are paid for under the Medicaid program) families will have no option but to attempt home care. For those who are unaware, Medicare does NOT pay for routine assistance with daily living tasks ( toileting, bathing, dressing, feeding). Medicare only pays for "skilled care" which is specific types of care and then only for about 90 days. Many families are shocked to discover this and even more shocked when they discover the costs of nursing home care or even assisted living care.
